So we'll begin with motivation and background,

and then we'll go into the four main corners of our project,

then implementation, how we did it,

and then how we evaluated how well the model did,

and then just the last conclusions and whatnot.

So first, let's start with motivation and background.

So the first challenge that motivated this project

is defining action spaces.

When you consider action spaces, you

have to look at continuous spaces and discrete spaces.

Each space has its own advantages,

but continuous spaces.

You have to read about computational efficiency

and suboptimal solutions.

But defining discrete spaces that are super flexible

is really tough, especially when you consider

that you have infinitely possible discrete actions,

but you need to define a space somehow.

Our next challenge is abstractly representing the environment.

So for planning, an agent needs to understand

what its environment actually is.

For humans, we can just say there's a tree,

or that's a computer, but an agent can't just do that.

So how do we turn these raw sensor data that we can give an agent

into meaningful ideas?

There are some issues with deep learning,

like it doesn't have an internal world model,

and it learns correlations.

And while deep learning scales by avoiding the structure,

active inference actually builds up a structure from the ground up.

And we want to combine deep learning scalability with active inference's

structured understanding for a good environment representation.

The last challenge that motivated this project is considering computational efficiency.

In robotics, offloading competition to GPUs or cloud servers is often not possible when you

consider issues like latency and bandwidth limits.

This requires us to consider edge processing or on-device processing.

Both deep learning and active inference are computationally intensive for various reasons.

And our goal with this project is to maintain the active inference

framework's interpretability and adaptability while maintaining real-time inference and control on edge devices.

To address these challenges, we combine four main concepts.

First, hierarchical collective inference.

As from the diagram on the left, the brain works hierarchically with higher level prediction and planning

and lower level perception modules.

Each layer updates beliefs to reduce prediction error.

Hierarchical active inference models this top-down, bottom-up like blue

by minimizing uncertainty at each level to guide adaptive behavior.

Information from lower levels is compressed into higher levels.

And you still retain these key concepts, which is the main thing which will address a lot of our challenges.

And it also allows, in practical use, computation can be distributed among multiple levels.

The next concept is a foreign theory.

A foreign theory was originally described in the context of ecological psychology

as abstract ideas offered by the environment that can restrict or assist possible actions.

However, this is very adjacent and can be directly used with the active inference framework

to enhance policy selection while contextualizing the environment.

We can take the concept of a foreign theory, which can replace traditional states with abstract ideas

and reducing the need for compute for inference.

And this serves as a complementary role as behavior emerges from interactions between the agent and the environment,

which bridges the abstract cognition with real-world context.

Another approach we took is the idea of scalable policy selection.

To scale policies, you need to have some sort of structure that can allow you to maintain low computation

while still scaling up the amount of policies you consider.

In our case, we use a latent suitability state in an affordance map.

What that essentially means is we have an affordance called suitability,

which encodes numerical or categorical evaluations of some region or path that the drone is about to take.

So as you can see in the diagram below, low suitability indicates high obstacle counts,

and it's not very desirable.

But high suitability means you have lower obstacle counts

and you're closer to the target location and the agent wants to be there.

This contextualization allows you to scale up policy selection because you can filter out those low suitability actions.

You don't need to consider them because they're just not suitable.

And this reduced policy set can be efficiently evaluated through EFE

without the need for some advanced hardware or off-sourcing compute.

The last area we need to consider is autonomous drone navigation.

So notoriously, autonomous drone navigation has a lot of different issues that we need to consider.

So just quickly going over some of them.

The first is that sensor data is often noisy, which makes perception and localization very tough challenges.

The next is that environmental factors like low light, glare, or clutter can additionally reduce our sensing's reliability.

After that, you have to consider that real-time adaptation to dynamic and uncertain environments is very difficult.

If you have a drone autonomously navigated in an unknown environment, it needs to be able to move and think very fast to adapt to brand new situations and issues that's never come across before.

After that, we have to think about the fact that there's limited interpretability and robustness in existing navigation algorithms.

We already talked about some of the issues with deep learning, but in the field of autonomous drone navigation, these issues are more and more important.

Then high computational load restricts deployment on low-power edge hardware.

Because in autonomous drone navigation, we're navigating environments where issues like latency are amplified.

Almost everything has to be on edge hardware, but this isn't always possible with the most frontier models.

Finally, there's often difficulty infusing multimodal data from LiDAR, camera, and IMU consistently.

We need to have all these different data sources to enable all the different elements of our navigation.

But diffuse them and provide them to the model is a challenge.

So now let's go into the actual architectural structure.

So we have a three-tiered architecture.

We have high-level inference and planning.

And these lower levels, all of this builds up and sets up the high-level planning to be as efficient and streamlined as possible.

We have our mid-level perception and encoding, which builds our world model.

It builds it from observations and its suitability and then creates a world model and beliefs about the world model, beliefs about the environment.

Finally, we have low-level control, which right now is very simple.

It just executes movement and records change in position to maintain egocentric navigation.

So let's go over these areas a bit more in-depth.

So first is perception, where we take environmental data and process it into a point cloud.

So you can see that we have the Aerosens simulation and we collect data from the LiDAR and camera, which are two primary methods of perception.

We turn these into structured observations, specifically target distance, azimuth elevation, and obstacle distance, obstacle density.

And we use these to update our world models' state base, with distance, azimuth elevation, and suitability here.

Then we take our affordances, which calculate expected suitability scores for the region around us, based on the same perception data we got from Microsoft AirSim.

Then we move to high-low planning, where we sample optimal policies from a filtered action space that's created based on our affordances.

And this high-low planning works to minimize expected free energy with its pragmatic and efficient component.

Finally, we do our final execution, setting it to a very simple low-level module for physical execution in Microsoft AirSim.

So now, let's talk about a factor graph of our architecture.

Our architecture is currently very simple, so there's not much special about our factor graph.

It's a typical message-passing structure between t and t plus 1, but there's a very key difference.

This S4, or suitability, actually feeds directly into the observation, and it dynamically weights that preference to then move into EFE.

We'll definitely talk about this more in detail once we get to the planning calculations, but this is a very key difference, and it highlights a key problem with our architecture.

Because it directly feeds in, it's actually completely deterministic, and there is no probability involved.

It's just, okay, I look at my information, I see this amount of suitability, that's my observation.

We need to make this probabilistic so that the drone doesn't have 100% trust in its sensors, because sensors are noisy, as we mentioned earlier.

Yeah, so that's definitely a feature improvement that we need to do.

Now let's go more in-depth onto the perception and world model area.

So the first thing to consider is the multimodal data acquisition.

As we mentioned earlier, our two primary methods are from the LiDAR and depth camera.

So a lot of it gives us distance data. The effective range we chose is from 0.1 to 50 meters, which is kind of arbitrary, but we found this based on testing in Microsoft AirSIM.

This is the effective range that we need for our purposes.

Then is the depth camera, which is only from 0.1 to 20 meters, and this gives us very accurate depth data.

This is an image you can see of what this some kind of sensor data would look like.

For us, what we decided to do was use a db scan algorithm to cluster all of the fuse points we get.

And we have an epsilon of 1.0 meters, which basically means that the max cluster size is 1.0 meters.

So in each area of 1.0 meters, you're going to cluster into one point and the min samples 2, meaning for one cluster, you need at least two points.

This process then abstractly represents the raw points into a discrete set of obstacles, and this internally performs denoising.

For example, if there was a random point somewhere in an individual point, then the db scan algorithm would ignore that because it's below min samples 2.

Then going more into this for preprocessing and fusion, we take our raw sensor data and filter it for some additional noise reduction and remove outer range points within the effective ranges we talked about.

And there's an optional subsampling in case computational efficiency means that we can't handle large amounts of data.

For fusion, we again just take both water data and camera data and transform it into a single unified coordinate frame,

which creates one large point cloud, which is more similar to the image you see here.

Then we do our data packaging and output for the technical part of it.

So, so far everything's been done on Python and we're calculating more information about the target, like angle, elevation, and distance.

This is mostly supplied by information from the Airstream function, which is kind of like the replacement for the ion view simulation.

Although obstacle data is also packaged here, we take the obstacle positions, which we got from db scan and the obstacle density, which is a very simple calculation that represents how dense the environment is.

And this information is then finally packaged into a JSON file and we use a ZMQ system to communicate between the Python and Julia areas of a project.

So all of this is in Python and ZMQ then sends it to Julia, which is going to go over our planning module and it's going to process it.

All right. So let's go into this actual suitability calculation.

So our suitability looks like this. We have a weight, we have safety factor, another weight, and then density factor.

The weights are just normalized to add to one. And then we have these weights here. These are hyper parameters.

There's a lot of room for improvement, which we'll talk about later.

So here's how the safety factor looks. It's an exponential where essentially, when d, the obstacle

distance, is very close to zero, you immediately know it's unsafe because it's an obstacle right in

front of me or right next to me. And our safety factor crashes as it's an exponential decay function.

So then as d approaches larger and larger numbers, it gets safer and safer and safer because the exponential

increases. And then we know, okay, I'm not close to an obstacle. Let's just ignore and let's keep going.

And then we also have this penalty, which is like, if there's an obstacle within 1.2 meters,

we replace safety or we multiply safety by an exponential negative 1 over d, which basically

says, okay, there's this obstacle that's some amount of close to me. Let's quantify that and then go from

there. So this is kind of a penalty for very unsafe environments. When there's a lot of obstacles, it

comes into play a lot. Then let's go to the density factor. It's based on an obstacle density. Both of these

are considered observations, 0.4 and 0.5, as we saw earlier. So we take the density factor, which is very

similarly structured. It's based on obstacle density. And then there's a cutoff for both. But for this

one, the cutoff is more noticeable. This is like just a density threshold that if it gets below this, it's

like it just, the density factor becomes 1 immediately, because we know, very safe. And then steepness is just

quantifies how sensitive it is to changes in density. So as density gets low, as you can probably guess,

the factor gets close to 1, so that, okay, I know it's quite safe. And then as density gets very high,

the factor gets lower to lower suitability. There's also another density penalty for high densities.

It's a bunch of stuff, but essentially it takes the maximum of a hyperparameter and then

a calculation with obstacle density and checks, okay, how much should I scale the factor to penalize this

factor so that suitability begins to lower? That eventually ends up with a normalized score

from 0 to 1, which represents this overall navigability and safety. Now you can clearly

see that there's a lot of rule-based elements that require hyperparameter sweeps. And that is a very,

very glaring problem with architecture that has a fairly arbitrary fix, which we'll definitely talk

about later. Let's go over the overall planning process. So we start with waypoint generation.

Once we have our internal suitability, we're able to do this, where we generate 25 to 75 candidate waypoints

based on obstacle density. So again, this is another example of the agent taking environmental context

and using it to inform its planning. We do a dual strategy where the sampling 70% about it is exploring

the nearby space. And there's some target bias sampling, an initial 30%, that's pushing the agent more

towards the goal. We have an adaptive radius, which is where the action length is between 1 to 10 meters,

which is again, based on internal suitability. So we use a very simple calculation where the minimum is 1,

and then you raise suitability to the power of 3, and multiply by 10 minus 1. So this basically means

as suitability approaches 0, then the action length would be approaching 1, and vice versa, suitability is

going really well towards 1, then our action length is going to be 10. There's an additional check that's

performed after this to ensure that we have at least enough to reach a target when you're in some area

that's nearby the target. So you can see from this whole process, you have flexible actions. If the

agent's in a very open space, then there's absolutely no need for it to be taking a small action. Like 1,

we'll be taking something more larger, like 7 or 8, and vice versa, if you're in very dense space,

we can take actions of when. And yeah. So let's go more into how this process works.

When we get this set of waypoints, we calculate the expected suitability. This is a very similar

calculation as Syphiky talked about, but the intro suitability, it just has a few additional

modifications and penalties. So one of them is that there's more conservative parameters used. We

pretty much use trigger cutoffs for distance and steepness, which just makes it a safer check.

We then add some additional cluster and octant penalties. So we check for any dense obstacle

clusters in any of the 8 octants around the waypoints. And if any octant is dense, which has

like 5 more than 5 obstacles, then its local density score is penalized. And if it's extremely dense,

like even more, and it has more than 10 obstacles, its local obstacle distance is also penalized.

We also have a few other penalties like corridor penalty, where the path from the current position

is checked for narrow gaps. And if the path is too narrow, then suitability is severely penalized.

This means that we're not just considering where the agent's going to end up, but the process of how

the agent's going to get there. Finally, we have this target proximity one is where, again, if the agent is

close to your target, the waypoints moving more directly towards it receive an additional suitability

boost. Yeah. And something to add is these local density score and local obstacle distance are the

same things as the factors that we talked about before. So we basically penalize or increase these

for expected suitability based on these factors. Finally, we filter. So after all the calculations,

the system discards all these waypoints with a score less than 8 to 0.8. And then you calculate

transitions for other states with a suitability of 0.95. And then we calculate EFE. So we evaluate EFE

for this action set by encoding paths with EFE based on their location and all these factors.

So our equation looks like this. We take in the current state, so distance, angle, and suitability,

and then the action, which is a 3D vector, and then the agent's current beliefs as probability

distributions. So we have the goal seeking behavior, the uncertainty reduction term, and the VFE term,

which essentially, we'll talk about later, but essentially uses VFE to penalize or increase EFE.

All right, so let's break down each part of EFE in each term. So first, the pragmatic term. It's just

a pragmatic weight. And then preference score, distance bonus, and this magnitude term. So

preference score is just your preferences for distance, angle, suitability, as a weighted average,

which we'll talk about in the next slide. The distance bonus, which is just your expected distance

minus the current target distance, and then factor of two. And finally, action magnitude. So larger

actions would incur greater scaling of this, because your pragmatic value should increase, or rather,

be minimized as you get closer to the target. So basically, in essence, a higher pragmatic value

would lead to more efficient actions, while the adaptive weighing does ensure some caveats in very

dense environments. Now let's go over the epistemic value. So just keep in mind, in general, in any

acumen-prince framework, the epistemic value is in charge of driving the agent to explore and reduce

uncertainty against beliefs. So for example, if the agent's like, for this state, I have a very low

certainty in it, what action can incentivize and help me find more information about the state? That's

what the epistemic value is encoding. So let's go over specifically in our architecture, what we have.

So the equation is very simple. We have this epistemic weight, which is by default 0.2. It's a hyperparameter.

And we do dynamically increase this in our system in high density areas, which promotes more

acceleration and looking around for safe paths. We then multiply by an additional factor of 0.5.

This is arbitrarily chosen by us. After that, we multiply by the total entropy, or which is

encoding the uncertainty of the agent's beliefs. This is computed as the equation h of q is equal to

h of the q of the location, plus h of q of our angle, plus h of q of our suitability. So in essence,

it's just calculating the total entropy of our states as per the argument's framework.

This is a more detailed equation as per the belief distribution for how the calculation works,

where you have the summation, and you take each part of the belief distribution, and add this term

epsilon to it, and take the log of that. It's very standard calculation. And finally, in this,

going back to the equation, we multiply it by the action magnitude. Again, if you remember, our actions

vary from length one to length 10. And this term just reflects that increased epistemic value for

the larger actions. You might think this is a bit strange, but logically, the motivation is,

if an agent is moving further, then it's able to uncover more information about the environment,

and thus, it has a greater epistemic value. Just note, because of the way things are set up with

the architecture, we are multiplying this by a negative, and our total EFT ends up being negative.

This is just how things are supposed to be. And overall, once we get this epistemic value,

we see that a higher epistemic value is going to incentivize the agent to reduce uncertainty about

its environment, thus balancing exploration and goal pursuit. Our final part of EFE is this BFE penalty.

This is a very non-traditional and not super necessary term, but it is still there, so we

decided to talk about it. It's a small regularization term that links our planning to the inference.

Because we don't have very explicit perceptual inference, we do this term. So lambda is just a

small fixed hyperparameter, which ensures the penalty is about 10% of EFE, so it doesn't really affect EFE

that much. And then VFE is just your variation for energy calculated during inference. It measures

how well current beliefs, explain the observation, essentially just our traditional EFE, VFE.

And we have complexity, or your entropy term, and then you have belief accuracy,

such as your expected probability of alteration over states, i.e. your likelihood. And then your prior

belief, just a simple belief updating system, which is how VFE is struggling.

Also, this all leads into control, which is just our final steps. These are simple errors and functions,

with a certain tool, it makes it more likely that you can achieve what you want to achieve in your

project. And then the coordination of goals. And the coordination of goals is I want to be able to

coordinate my goals across the team. I don't want to just do things on my own and then someone else do

things on their own and then combine those at the end. We want to coordinate our efforts

in an active way. We want to be able to have this sort of collective cognition where we can,

an environment where we can do that. So when we define our different terms here in terms of what

So ultimately, these agents are trying to solve different collective action problems.

So, you know, there are different aspects to this, and we, but we don't want to just model

collective action for the sake of modeling collective action. We want to map this to

some process in open source. And so here we have branch development in Git, and we have pull requests.

So we have different branches where different branches are created for different purposes by

different agents, and they're all kind of pushing their work to a main branch where they're, you know,

they're kind of taking the work from the main branch, creating their own branch, finishing their work,

and then issuing a pull request back to the main branch.

So this is collective action because all the agents have to coordinate their activities so that at the

end, the main branch has like a coherent project, and it isn't just, you know, everything that's

pushed, smooshed together, and it's not workable. So, you know, there are a bunch of things that drive

collective action in this context. So the first is internal motivation. A contributor wants to work

on an issue. So if a contributor doesn't want to work on an issue, they won't bother making a branch,

and they certainly won't bother making a pull request. There also has to be cooperation. So

sometimes agents can get together, make their own branches, and use a divide and conquer strategy

to sort of address the available tasks and address what needs to be done. They coordinate their efforts

through looking at these diffs, which are the differences between different branches,

and addressing those accordingly. And then that leads to this finished project. So you have this

sort of technological layer that allows us to make these branches, compare these diffs, and do this

on an automated fashion, which helps with the cognitive load. But it's also important to have the sort of

internal motivation and this cooperative aspect of the behavior. And then, of course, there's the external

motivation, which is sort of addressing a technical layer, which is that we have to make contributing to

this project seamless and meaningful. So if the project is not, you know, easy to contribute to, and it isn't

really about anything, then people won't bother to do any of this. Another process we can model is the issue queue, or the

Kanban board. So Kanban boards are used ubiquitously in project management. Issue queues are also used to

store issues, to move issues through different types of different levels of priority from beginning to

completion. And so there are a lot of things that drive this at the individual level, at the team level,

and then sort of at the issue creation level itself. So individual prioritization can be characterized as

what issues contributors should address first, later, and last. So when we build a Kanban board,

we usually create these categories of, you know, to do, in progress, urgent, and finished. And we have to

decide as individuals where to put those issues. What's the state of completion for an issue, and where

do we put it? So, you know, maybe we don't want to work on certain issues at all. Maybe we find other issues

to be very appealing, or maybe we take all of our issues and we move very slowly through them. Maybe we

move faster. And, but we have to have this means of sort of addressing issues. Sometimes we procrastinate

on them. And, you know, we have to have this model of moving on things so that things get completed.

Then we have team prioritization. So in team prioritization, we can ask what issues are at

what stage in project development. So if we have a team reviewing a Kanban board, that team will look at

all the individuals and their collective prioritization techniques. And then they will

just basically say, okay, this is what the state of the project is. And I'm going to give feedback

to the individuals to, or we're going to give feedback to the individuals to say what they need

to do to improve these. And then issue creation is basically what is the fundamental unit of work.

So all of our individuals must have be in agreement on the same, roughly the same fundamental unit of

work, how big are your issues. And the team also has to be in agreement or else you have a lot of

conflict. You have a lot of fighting over, you know, what exactly it needs to be done, how much work needs

We can just make that assumption, but we don't know the details of it. You know, maybe they're doing it for

a class project, which is motivation, but it's not the same as being like kind of motivated by the problem.

And that's the problem. We don't really have a lot more information to go on than that. And so

simulations based on these indirect measurements may not accurately reflect the dynamics we find in

collective behavior as a multi-arm bandit problem. Basically, a multi-arm bandit, the name of the

multi-arm bandit comes from when people pull the lever of a slot machine. So you're like priming

this stochastic process. And you'll have like in front of you, if you've ever gone to a casino,

you'll see people sometimes put like five slot machines at once, and they'll pull the arms down

and then see what they get. That's what you're doing in a multi-arm bandit. And you're trying to

figure out whether you should explore a space or exploit a space. So if you think about that in

terms of issues, should you explore an issue deeply or should you explore more issues in a more shallow

way? We implement this in different ways so that we can look at that as a sort of a factor for driving

open source project behavior. The second point is building a statistical description of the mind. So

this is of course is the internal components or the internal model of each agent. So we want to build

a statistical description of that mind. We want to give it beliefs and we want to have, you know,

have each agent minimize its own free energy. And so this is something that of course, we also want

the agents to sort of be able to figure out what other agents are thinking to so that they can match

their beliefs and minimize their free energy together. So this is kind of where we're going with this

in terms of the collective part of it. And then of course, when we reduce our error,

it reinforces causal relationships that are necessary to complete the project. So getting the agents to see

what the causal relationships are between different parts of the project. If we can reduce their

inferential error, then we can get all the agents to move in the same direction.

So this was implemented using an inter-inforactively. So this is the link to interactively. It was used

to do two things. Number one is to implement decision problems in a single agent, as we said. So each of

these agents have some decision process going on inside of them. And then two is to implement a theory

of mind to infer the state of neighboring agents. So the agent on the left is trying to characterize the

state of the agent on the right, because the agent on the left doesn't know what the agent on the right

is thinking. And if they're both thinking the same thing, they're able to maximize utility in the same

way, then they'll be able to cooperate better and do things like divide and conquer. So this is where you

have this agent is asking this other agent, is this what you're thinking? And they say, yes.

that's a little bit harder to determine. So let's walk through some of these methods

for reinforcement learning to see maybe how they compare with active inference. So we start with Q-learning.

So for any Markov decision process, we identify optimal action selection policy. So what we want

to do with that action selection policy is maximize the expected value of the total reward.

Okay. So then this determines the quality of a given policy. An action A sub T with respect to state S

of T provided a learning rate of alpha. So we have this equation down here where we

have these different components. We have alpha times Q, where we're evaluating the action and the state,

the quality of each action and state that evaluates the current action selection value.

We add that to this estimate of optimal future values, where the state of highest quality,

and so we're looking for the maximum quality. And then that's our kind of the way, the direction

we're going in Q-learning. We're trying to find that maximum feature value, the maximum quality

of a given set of policies. Now, the multi-arm bandit is also used then in reinforcement learning,

like we saw with active inference. It's used for task allocation, role matching given prior performance.

It encourages specialization while allowing for flexibility. And it utilizes what we call on-policy

temporal difference learning, which we'll get into next. And so it's important to recall the different

community member types, maintainer, contributor, innovator, knowledge curator. And we can use this

to think about how to allocate different tasks to these different classes of a contributor,

or these different classes of project member. So temporal difference learning can be stated as a state

value function of a finite state Markov decision process under a policy pi. V sub pi is equal to the

state value function of the Markov decision process with states, rewards, and a discount rate,

importance, which states the importance of future rewards. So we have, instead of our learning rate,

we have a discount rate. And then we estimate V pi s, which is the function for the state value. And we

evaluate this policy pi, we obtain the reward r, and we update our value function. Now, some of the metrics

that were created in the reinforcement learning work, and this was done by VD Ruhira, she developed five

different metrics that work in this context. So the first is the harmony index. So remember, I said there

are different things we want to measure. And then there are different things that we can sort of have

as like an output. So the first the harmony index, this captures workload balance and tasks success rate.

This is basically measures how well balanced workflow workloads are, how much cognitive load there is

for each agent, and then the success of agents on those tasks. Then there's the resilience quotient,

which measures adaptability during disruptions, such as dropouts and workload spikes. It means basically

how resilient are you to different types of, you know, disruptions in your work in the project.

There's reassignment overhead, which tracks inefficiency caused by reassigning tasks.

There's an initial, an initial density of agents. So basically, it's where if you have a dense setting

of agents to start with, it's going to give you a very different result than a very, very small number

of agents. And we can use that to sort of normalize a lot of the heterogeneity in open source projects.

And then the contributor maintainer vision. So if we don't have a good vision, agents won't know if

there's anything for them to do. But if the vision is there, then at least they have in their mind

sort of a mental model that they have something to do. And so they go to find specific tasks to do,

to solve specific issues. The next thing I want to talk about is how large language models were used.

technical skills. So Linus Torvalds is the benevolent dictator in the Linux example. And then the

they can in the project. I want to get into the way we're thinking about integrating a lot of these

interesting thing, because none of those are described by any of the models that we've talked about.

So this is a point for future work. We also know that like some of these dynamics, because you get

a lot of people leaving a lot of people joining, a lot of flux and churn, that it can create vulnerabilities,

as well as opportunities. And so if we want to know how to sort of build a model that can help us

understand maybe what those opportunities are, those vulnerabilities are, there are some ways forward.

So one way is to build these sort of what we call meta brain models, where you have different,

you take different models, and you put them together, different layers of different types

of representations, put them together as a structure that sort of, you know, communicates selectively

working together on tasks that are semantically defined, and then maybe an agent based model, and

sort of the driver of collective behavior. So you have this agent based model, sort of coordinating all

these different brains, composed of large language models, multi agent reinforcement learning, active

inference models.

So I want to finish with sort of a thought from Jesse Perrin, and he's another person in our lab who's

contributed to this work. And he's been thinking a lot about project management, and a lot about how

we often create tools that don't go anywhere, or we have like initiatives that kind of die, because no one finds them relevant.

So he says sustainability translates to problems in academic communities. Sometimes if you go to an academic

community, you find that someone earns like a grant for tool building, so they get funding for tool building,

they build these great tools, and then the funding runs out. And so they have this open source tool that

they put out in the world, the funding runs out, and it becomes an unmaintained tool, eventually to die

a premature death. So that's one path that's suboptimal, I think, from the standpoint of open source

sustainability, where you get money to build something, you build it, and then it dies.

The other example that he gives is that someone builds a tool, and because it's not really relevant to

anything, the tool is ignored and not maintained. And so then that's bad, but it gets worse because then

someone else builds a similar tool, and that dies because it's ignored and not maintained. So you end

up with this graveyard of tools that are all maybe pretty decent, but because no one's finding them

relevant, and no one's putting the money into maintenance, the people keep reinventing the wheel,

and then the wheel breaks apart. So that's not good. So, you know, Jesse makes this observation

that academic communities more generally will often build out infrastructure, which goes nowhere,

and is disconnected from real world impact. So that's something to think about if we're thinking

about the Active Inference Institute, or our own lab, or other institutions where people build

infrastructure, it goes nowhere, or it's not connected to the real world in relevant ways. And sometimes

you just can't, you know, you can't avoid it, because you don't necessarily know what people will find

relevant. But that's part of sustainability that we haven't addressed, and maybe is sort of the secret
